```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Test-time compute scaling has emerged as a complement to model scaling \cite{snell2025scaling}: repeated sampling with majority voting \citep{wang2023selfconsistency}, best-of-$N$ with learned verifiers \cite{cobbe2021verifiers, brown2024monkeys}, and extended chain-of-thought \cite{muennighoff2025s1} yield large gains on reasoning and code generation, but the evidence concentrates on tasks with discrete answers or execution-based verification.
4	Named entity recognition (NER) poses a structurally different challenge: the output is a \textit{set} of entities, two samples may extract overlapping but non-identical sets, and there is no obvious majority to vote on.
5	This challenge generalizes to any \textit{set-valued} structured prediction task where outputs admit partial but not exact overlap.
6	
7	Prior work on confidence estimation for LLM-based IE has conflated \textit{quality estimation} (which inputs does the model handle reliably?) with \textit{sample selection} (which of $N$ outputs is best?).
8	Existing self-consistency methods \cite{wang2023selfconsistency, chen2024usc, taubenfeld2025cisc, kang2025selfcertainty} assume outputs can be grouped by equivalence, an assumption that breaks for entity sets with partial overlap.
9	Unlike \citet{yao2026consistency}, who demonstrate that self-consistency correlates with task accuracy, we provide the first systematic empirical characterization of \textit{when and why} test-time scaling via LP-based selection succeeds or fails for structured prediction, diagnosing degeneracy and compression as the two blocking mechanisms.
10	
11	A key distinction structures our analysis: four of five signals (Soft Jaccard, voting confidence, Fleiss' $\kappa$, exact match) are instance-level aggregates that assign identical scores to all $N$ candidates for a given input; only log-probability (LP) is natively per-sample.
12	All signals achieve reliable quality estimation ($\rho$ up to 0.45, AUROC up to 0.82; Figure~\ref{fig:overview}), supporting selective prediction.
13	For selection, LP achieves $+1.39$~pp on Few-NERD ($p{<}0.001$) in settings where both low instance degeneracy and adequate alignment are present, with gains scaling with $N$.
14	On SciERC, the observed LP gain ($+0.2$~pp, $p{=}0.29$) falls below the minimum detectable effect (${\approx}$1.5~pp at 80\% power, $n{=}529$) despite 13~pp oracle headroom. We term this disparity between quality estimation strength and selection effectiveness the \textbf{correlation-selection gap}.
15	Three factors qualitatively shape this gap: (a)~\textit{instance degeneracy} (13\textendash 68\% across our benchmarks) compresses the candidate space; (b)~\textit{oracle headroom} bounds the achievable gain; (c)~\textit{LP-quality alignment} (within-instance $\rho(\text{LP}, \text{F1})$) determines whether LP can discriminate candidates in non-degenerate instances (\S\ref{sec:selection_gap}).
16	Oracle F1 scales with $N$ at rates comparable to reasoning tasks ($c{\approx}0.5$; \citealp{brown2024monkeys}), yet confidence-based aggregation recovers ${<}1$\,pp of the growing ceiling---a persistent \textit{aggregation gap} that intensifies with model capability as degeneracy rises with training convergence (\S\ref{sec:scaling}).
17	Our diagnosis complements MBR utility misalignment \cite{eikema2025structured}: degeneracy eliminates candidate diversity before utility alignment matters (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
18	
19	\begin{figure*}[t]
20	\centering
21	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_overview}
22	\caption{Overview of the correlation-selection gap. \textbf{Left}: consistency signals estimate instance quality ($\rho$ up to 0.45; Table~\ref{tab:quality_estimation}). \textbf{Center}: instance-level signals assign identical scores to all $N$ candidates, so selection cannot outperform greedy. \textbf{Right}: entity-level construction via majority vote ($\theta{=}2/N$) bypasses the bottleneck ($+1.40$\,pp on Few-NERD; \S\ref{sec:prescriptive}).}
23	\label{fig:overview}
24	\end{figure*}
25	
26	We evaluate five signals on four NER benchmarks (SciERC \cite{luan2018scierc}, Few-NERD \cite{ding2021fewnerd}, CoNLL-2003 \cite{tjongkimsang2003conll}, WNUT-17 \cite{derczynski2017wnut}) spanning a difficulty gradient, using two 8B models (Qwen3-8B, LLaMA-3.1-8B) with a 4B ablation, varying $N{\in}\{2,\ldots,32\}$ and model capacity across 4B\textendash 8B fine-tuned and 72B zero-shot regimes.
27	Our contributions:
28	\begin{enumerate}
29	    \item \textbf{Quality estimation as a test-time scaling diagnostic.}
30	    Five signals from three families evaluated on four NER benchmarks, two model families, and a 4B ablation, achieving reliable quality estimation ($\rho$ up to 0.45, AUROC up to 0.82).
31	    QE enables selective prediction: filtering low-confidence instances at 80\% coverage yields $+5$--$8$\,pp F1 (AUPRC 0.70--0.92, VC strongest; \S\ref{sec:prescriptive}).
32	
33	    \item \textbf{Failure modes of test-time scaling for structured prediction.}
34	    For fine-tuned 4B--8B models, LP-quality alignment (within-instance $\rho(\text{LP}, \text{F1})$) is the primary predictor of selection success: LP achieves $+1.39$~pp on Few-NERD ($p{<}0.001$) where alignment is strong, but not on benchmarks with weak alignment.
35	    Instance degeneracy causally modulates LP within fixed settings (epoch and temperature ablations) but does not predict cross-dataset direction ($\rho{=}0.052$, 23 configurations); supervised combinations and hidden state probes (Appendix~\ref{app:hidden_probe}) confirm the gap persists.
36	    The resulting three-step diagnostic (measure degeneracy, assess signal alignment, choose aggregation) provides a reusable protocol for structured test-time scaling (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
37	
38	    \item \textbf{Entity-level construction as a partial mitigation for structured test-time scaling failures.}
39	    The diagnostic naturally motivates entity-level aggregation, adapting entity voting from ensemble NER \cite{florian2003named} to stochastic sampling: under fine-tuning and zero-shot regimes, LP-weighted construction yields $+1.40$\,pp on Few-NERD ($p{<}0.001$; survives Holm--Bonferroni, $k{=}57$) and $+1.02$\,pp on SciERC ($p{=}0.004$, $p_{\text{adj}}{=}0.20$; \S\ref{sec:prescriptive}).
40	    Construction and LP selection are complementary, governed by LP-quality alignment.
41	\end{enumerate}
42	
43

      "content": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nTest-time compute scaling has emerged as a complement to model scaling \\cite{snell2025scaling}: repeated sampling with majority voting \\citep{wang2023selfconsistency}, best-of-$N$ with learned verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers, brown2024monkeys}, and extended chain-of-thought \\cite{muennighoff2025s1} yield large gains on reasoning and code generation, but the evidence concentrates on tasks with discrete answers or execution-based verification.\nNamed entity recognition (NER) poses a structurally different challenge: the output is a \\textit{set} of entities, two samples may extract overlapping but non-identical sets, and there is no obvious majority to vote on.\nThis challenge generalizes to any \\textit{set-valued} structured prediction task where outputs admit partial but not exact overlap.\n\nPrior work on confidence estimation for LLM-based IE has conflated \\textit{quality estimation} (which inputs does the model handle reliably?) with \\textit{sample selection} (which of $N$ outputs is best?).\nExisting self-consistency methods \\cite{wang2023selfconsistency, chen2024usc, taubenfeld2025cisc, kang2025selfcertainty} assume outputs can be grouped by equivalence, an assumption that breaks for entity sets with partial overlap.\nUnlike \\citet{yao2026consistency}, who demonstrate that self-consistency correlates with task accuracy, we provide the first systematic empirical characterization of \\textit{when and why} test-time scaling via LP-based selection succeeds or fails for structured prediction, diagnosing degeneracy and compression as the two blocking mechanisms.\n\nA key distinction structures our analysis: four of five signals (Soft Jaccard, voting confidence, Fleiss' $\\kappa$, exact match) are instance-level aggregates that assign identical scores to all $N$ candidates for a given input; only log-probability (LP) is natively per-sample.\nAll signals achieve reliable quality estimation ($\\rho$ up to 0.45, AUROC up to 0.82; Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}), supporting selective prediction.\nFor selection, LP achieves $+1.39$~pp on Few-NERD ($p{<}0.001$) in settings where both low instance degeneracy and adequate alignment are present, with gains scaling with $N$.\nOn SciERC, the observed LP gain ($+0.2$~pp, $p{=}0.29$) falls below the minimum detectable effect (${\\approx}$1.5~pp at 80\\% power, $n{=}529$) despite 13~pp oracle headroom. We term this disparity between quality estimation strength and selection effectiveness the \\textbf{correlation-selection gap}.\nThree factors qualitatively shape this gap: (a)~\\textit{instance degeneracy} (13\\textendash 68\\% across our benchmarks) compresses the candidate space; (b)~\\textit{oracle headroom} bounds the achievable gain; (c)~\\textit{LP-quality alignment} (within-instance $\\rho(\\text{LP}, \\text{F1})$) determines whether LP can discriminate candidates in non-degenerate instances (\\S\\ref{sec:selection_gap}).\nOracle F1 scales with $N$ at rates comparable to reasoning tasks ($c{\\approx}0.5$; \\citealp{brown2024monkeys}), yet confidence-based aggregation recovers ${<}1$\\,pp of the growing ceiling---a persistent \\textit{aggregation gap} that intensifies with model capability as degeneracy rises with training convergence (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\nOur diagnosis complements MBR utility misalignment \\cite{eikema2025structured}: degeneracy eliminates candidate diversity before utility alignment matters (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_overview}\n\\caption{Overview of the correlation-selection gap. \\textbf{Left}: consistency signals estimate instance quality ($\\rho$ up to 0.45; Table~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation}). \\textbf{Center}: instance-level signals assign identical scores to all $N$ candidates, so selection cannot outperform greedy. \\textbf{Right}: entity-level construction via majority vote ($\\theta{=}2/N$) bypasses the bottleneck ($+1.40$\\,pp on Few-NERD; \\S\\ref{sec:prescriptive}).}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nWe evaluate five signals on four NER benchmarks (SciERC \\cite{luan2018scierc}, Few-NERD \\cite{ding2021fewnerd}, CoNLL-2003 \\cite{tjongkimsang2003conll}, WNUT-17 \\cite{derczynski2017wnut}) spanning a difficulty gradient, using two 8B models (Qwen3-8B, LLaMA-3.1-8B) with a 4B ablation, varying $N{\\in}\\{2,\\ldots,32\\}$ and model capacity across 4B\\textendash 8B fine-tuned and 72B zero-shot regimes.\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Quality estimation as a test-time scaling diagnostic.}\n    Five signals from three families evaluated on four NER benchmarks, two model families, and a 4B ablation, achieving reliable quality estimation ($\\rho$ up to 0.45, AUROC up to 0.82).\n    QE enables selective prediction: filtering low-confidence instances at 80\\% coverage yields $+5$--$8$\\,pp F1 (AUPRC 0.70--0.92, VC strongest; \\S\\ref{sec:prescriptive}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Failure modes of test-time scaling for structured prediction.}\n    For fine-tuned 4B--8B models, LP-quality alignment (within-instance $\\rho(\\text{LP}, \\text{F1})$) is the primary predictor of selection success: LP achieves $+1.39$~pp on Few-NERD ($p{<}0.001$) where alignment is strong, but not on benchmarks with weak alignment.\n    Instance degeneracy causally modulates LP within fixed settings (epoch and temperature ablations) but does not predict cross-dataset direction ($\\rho{=}0.052$, 23 configurations); supervised combinations and hidden state probes (Appendix~\\ref{app:hidden_probe}) confirm the gap persists.\n    The resulting three-step diagnostic (measure degeneracy, assess signal alignment, choose aggregation) provides a reusable protocol for structured test-time scaling (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Entity-level construction as a partial mitigation for structured test-time scaling failures.}\n    The diagnostic naturally motivates entity-level aggregation, adapting entity voting from ensemble NER \\cite{florian2003named} to stochastic sampling: under fine-tuning and zero-shot regimes, LP-weighted construction yields $+1.40$\\,pp on Few-NERD ($p{<}0.001$; survives Holm--Bonferroni, $k{=}57$) and $+1.02$\\,pp on SciERC ($p{=}0.004$, $p_{\\text{adj}}{=}0.20$; \\S\\ref{sec:prescriptive}).\n    Construction and LP selection are complementary, governed by LP-quality alignment.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n",
      "numLines": 43,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 43
    }
  },

### Tool result

_source line 7611 · 2026-05-20T11:37:16.530Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01GH6GG8Tx9XXJ1kaf6rz6dm`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Test-time compute scaling---repeated sampling with selection or aggregation---yields large gains on discrete-answer reasoning and code generation, but named entity recognition (NER) produces \textit{sets} of entities with partial overlap, where standard aggregation mechanisms break down.
3	We evaluate five confidence signals for quality estimation (QE) and sample selection on four NER benchmarks across fine-tuned 4B\textendash 8B and zero-shot 72B models.
4	All signals achieve reliable QE ($\rho$ up to 0.45 on CoNLL-2003, area under the ROC curve [AUROC] up to 0.82), enabling selective prediction ($+5$--$8$\,pp F1 at 80\% coverage).
5	For instance-level selection, log-probability (LP) succeeds only where LP-quality alignment is strong, and gains are absent elsewhere despite large oracle headroom (the \textbf{correlation-selection gap}).
6	We diagnose three structural failure modes---instance degeneracy, limited oracle headroom, and LP compression---that persist at 72B zero-shot scale, providing the first systematic characterization of when test-time scaling succeeds or fails for structured prediction.
7	The diagnostic motivates entity-level \textit{construction}, adapting entity voting to stochastic sampling: LP-weighted aggregation improves F1 by $+1.40$\,pp on Few-NERD and $+1.02$\,pp on SciERC under fine-tuning (multi-seed mean, $p{<}0.005$). Construction and LP selection are complementary, governed by LP-quality alignment.
8	\end{abstract}
9	
```

</details>

  "content": "\\section{Theoretical Analysis}\\label{sec:theory}\n\nWe formalize the observation that degeneracy bounds any SC method's gain.\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Degeneracy Upper Bound]\\label{thm:degeneracy_bound}\nFor any aggregation method $A$ mapping $N$ samples to a single output, $\\mathbb{E}[\\Delta F_1(A)] \\leq (1 - D) \\cdot G_{\\textup{nd}}$,\nwhere $D$ is the degeneracy rate and $G_{\\textup{nd}}$ the oracle gap on non-degenerate instances.\nFor signal-based methods with alignment $\\alpha \\in [0,1]$ (fraction of oracle gap recovered on non-degenerate instances), this tightens to $(1 - D) \\cdot G_{\\textup{nd}} \\cdot \\alpha$ (proof in Appendix~\\ref{app:bound_proof}).\n\\end{theorem}\n\nWith empirical $G_{\\textup{nd}}{\\approx}14.8$\\,pp and $\\alpha{\\approx}0.068$, the critical threshold is $\\theta_{\\textup{crit}}{=}0.70$: when $D{>}70$\\%, the bound falls below $\\varepsilon{=}0.3$\\,pp (Figure~\\ref{fig:bound}).\nPractitioners can skip SC when degeneracy exceeds this threshold.\nAcross 16 configurations, all gains respect the bound (ratio 3--12\\%); the bound is loose because $\\alpha$---not $D$---is the bottleneck.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_bound}\n\\caption{SC gain vs.\\ degeneracy across 16 configurations. Red: signal-adjusted bound ($\\alpha{=}0.068$); vertical dashed: $\\theta_{\\textup{crit}}{=}0.70$; horizontal dotted: $\\varepsilon{=}0.3$\\,pp significance threshold. All positive gains fall within the bound (right panel: ratio to weak bound, all ${<}0.13$).}\n\\label{fig:bound}\n\\end{figure}\n"
}

    "content": "\\section{Theoretical Analysis}\\label{sec:theory}\n\nWe formalize the observation that degeneracy bounds any SC method's gain.\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Degeneracy Upper Bound]\\label{thm:degeneracy_bound}\nFor any aggregation method $A$ mapping $N$ samples to a single output, $\\mathbb{E}[\\Delta F_1(A)] \\leq (1 - D) \\cdot G_{\\textup{nd}}$,\nwhere $D$ is the degeneracy rate and $G_{\\textup{nd}}$ the oracle gap on non-degenerate instances.\nFor signal-based methods with alignment $\\alpha \\in [0,1]$ (fraction of oracle gap recovered on non-degenerate instances), this tightens to $(1 - D) \\cdot G_{\\textup{nd}} \\cdot \\alpha$ (proof in Appendix~\\ref{app:bound_proof}).\n\\end{theorem}\n\nWith empirical $G_{\\textup{nd}}{\\approx}14.8$\\,pp and $\\alpha{\\approx}0.068$, the critical threshold is $\\theta_{\\textup{crit}}{=}0.70$: when $D{>}70$\\%, the bound falls below $\\varepsilon{=}0.3$\\,pp (Figure~\\ref{fig:bound}).\nPractitioners can skip SC when degeneracy exceeds this threshold.\nAcross 16 configurations, all gains respect the bound (ratio 3--12\\%); the bound is loose because $\\alpha$---not $D$---is the bottleneck.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_bound}\n\\caption{SC gain vs.\\ degeneracy across 16 configurations. Red: signal-adjusted bound ($\\alpha{=}0.068$); vertical dashed: $\\theta_{\\textup{crit}}{=}0.70$; horizontal dotted: $\\varepsilon{=}0.3$\\,pp significance threshold. All positive gains fall within the bound (right panel: ratio to weak bound, all ${<}0.13$).}\n\\label{fig:bound}\n\\end{figure}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [],
    "originalFile": null,
    "userModified": false
  },
  "sourceToolAssistantUUID": "28ab6ca1-c64e-4ef6-97d0-b6824e5ba001",

    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nTest-time compute scaling---repeated sampling with selection or aggregation---yields large gains on discrete-answer reasoning and code generation, but named entity recognition (NER) produces \\textit{sets} of entities with partial overlap, where standard aggregation mechanisms break down.\nWe evaluate five confidence signals for quality estimation (QE) and sample selection on four NER benchmarks across fine-tuned 4B\\textendash 8B and zero-shot 72B models.\nAll signals achieve reliable QE ($\\rho$ up to 0.45 on CoNLL-2003, area under the ROC curve [AUROC] up to 0.82), enabling selective prediction ($+5$--$8$\\,pp F1 at 80\\% coverage).\nFor instance-level selection, log-probability (LP) succeeds only where LP-quality alignment is strong, and gains are absent elsewhere despite large oracle headroom (the \\textbf{correlation-selection gap}).\nWe diagnose three structural failure modes---instance degeneracy, limited oracle headroom, and LP compression---that persist at 72B zero-shot scale, providing the first systematic characterization of when test-time scaling succeeds or fails for structured prediction.\nThe diagnostic motivates entity-level \\textit{construction}, adapting entity voting to stochastic sampling: LP-weighted aggregation improves F1 by $+1.40$\\,pp on Few-NERD and $+1.02$\\,pp on SciERC under fine-tuning (multi-seed mean, $p{<}0.005$). Construction and LP selection are complementary, governed by LP-quality alignment.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

Construction and LP selection are complementary, governed by LP-quality alignment.",

    "oldString": "    Construction and LP selection are complementary, governed by LP-quality alignment.\n\\end{enumerate}",
    "newString": "    Construction and LP selection are complementary, governed by LP-quality alignment.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{A degeneracy upper bound on SC gain.}\n    We prove that $\\mathbb{E}[\\Delta F_1] \\leq (1{-}D) \\cdot G_{\\text{nd}} \\cdot \\alpha$, yielding a critical threshold $\\theta_{\\text{crit}}{=}0.70$ above which SC cannot yield significant improvement (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}).\n\\end{enumerate}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nTest-time compute scaling has emerged as a complement to model scaling \\cite{snell2025scaling}: repeated sampling with majority voting \\citep{wang2023selfconsistency}, best-of-$N$ with learned verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers, brown2024monkeys}, and extended chain-of-thought \\cite{muennighoff2025s1} yield large gains on reasoning and code generation, but the evidence concentrates on tasks with discrete answers or execution-based verification.\nNamed entity recognition (NER) poses a structurally different challenge: the output is a \\textit{set} of entities, two samples may extract overlapping but non-identical sets, and there is no obvious majority to vote on.\nThis challenge generalizes to any \\textit{set-valued} structured prediction task where outputs admit partial but not exact overlap.\n\nPrior work on confidence estimation for LLM-based IE has conflated \\textit{quality estimation} (which inputs does the model handle reliably?) with \\textit{sample selection} (which of $N$ outputs is best?).\nExisting self-consistency methods \\cite{wang2023selfconsistency, chen2024usc, taubenfeld2025cisc, kang2025selfcertainty} assume outputs can be grouped by equivalence, an assumption that breaks for entity sets with partial overlap.\nUnlike \\citet{yao2026consistency}, who demonstrate that self-consistency correlates with task accuracy, we provide the first systematic empirical characterization of \\textit{when and why} test-time scaling via LP-based selection succeeds or fails for structured prediction, diagnosing degeneracy and compression as the two blocking mechanisms.\n\nA key distinction structures our analysis: four of five signals (Soft Jaccard, voting confidence, Fleiss' $\\kappa$, exact match) are instance-level aggregates that assign identical scores to all $N$ candidates for a given input; only log-probability (LP) is natively per-sample.\nAll signals achieve reliable quality estimation ($\\rho$ up to 0.45, AUROC up to 0.82; Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}), supporting selective prediction.\nFor selection, LP achieves $+1.39$~pp on Few-NERD ($p{<}0.001$) in settings where both low instance degeneracy and adequate alignment are present, with gains scaling with $N$.\nOn SciERC, the observed LP gain ($+0.2$~pp, $p{=}0.29$) falls below the minimum detectable effect (${\\approx}$1.5~pp at 80\\% power, $n{=}529$) despite 13~pp oracle headroom. We term this disparity between quality estimation strength and selection effectiveness the \\textbf{correlation-selection gap}.\nThree factors qualitatively shape this gap: (a)~\\textit{instance degeneracy} (13\\textendash 68\\% across our benchmarks) compresses the candidate space; (b)~\\textit{oracle headroom} bounds the achievable gain; (c)~\\textit{LP-quality alignment} (within-instance $\\rho(\\text{LP}, \\text{F1})$) determines whether LP can discriminate candidates in non-degenerate instances (\\S\\ref{sec:selection_gap}).\nOracle F1 scales with $N$ at rates comparable to reasoning tasks ($c{\\approx}0.5$; \\citealp{brown2024monkeys}), yet confidence-based aggregation recovers ${<}1$\\,pp of the growing ceiling---a persistent \\textit{aggregation gap} that intensifies with model capability as degeneracy rises with training convergence (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\nOur diagnosis complements MBR utility misalignment \\cite{eikema2025structured}: degeneracy eliminates candidate diversity before utility alignment matters (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_overview}\n\\caption{Overview of the correlation-selection gap. \\textbf{Left}: consistency signals estimate instance quality ($\\rho$ up to 0.45; Table~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation}). \\textbf{Center}: instance-level signals assign identical scores to all $N$ candidates, so selection cannot outperform greedy. \\textbf{Right}: entity-level construction via majority vote ($\\theta{=}2/N$) bypasses the bottleneck ($+1.40$\\,pp on Few-NERD; \\S\\ref{sec:prescriptive}).}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nWe evaluate five signals on four NER benchmarks (SciERC \\cite{luan2018scierc}, Few-NERD \\cite{ding2021fewnerd}, CoNLL-2003 \\cite{tjongkimsang2003conll}, WNUT-17 \\cite{derczynski2017wnut}) spanning a difficulty gradient, using two 8B models (Qwen3-8B, LLaMA-3.1-8B) with a 4B ablation, varying $N{\\in}\\{2,\\ldots,32\\}$ and model capacity across 4B\\textendash 8B fine-tuned and 72B zero-shot regimes.\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Quality estimation as a test-time scaling diagnostic.}\n    Five signals from three families evaluated on four NER benchmarks, two model families, and a 4B ablation, achieving reliable quality estimation ($\\rho$ up to 0.45, AUROC up to 0.82).\n    QE enables selective prediction: filtering low-confidence instances at 80\\% coverage yields $+5$--$8$\\,pp F1 (AUPRC 0.70--0.92, VC strongest; \\S\\ref{sec:prescriptive}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Failure modes of test-time scaling for structured prediction.}\n    For fine-tuned 4B--8B models, LP-quality alignment (within-instance $\\rho(\\text{LP}, \\text{F1})$) is the primary predictor of selection success: LP achieves $+1.39$~pp on Few-NERD ($p{<}0.001$) where alignment is strong, but not on benchmarks with weak alignment.\n    Instance degeneracy causally modulates LP within fixed settings (epoch and temperature ablations) but does not predict cross-dataset direction ($\\rho{=}0.052$, 23 configurations); supervised combinations and hidden state probes (Appendix~\\ref{app:hidden_probe}) confirm the gap persists.\n    The resulting three-step diagnostic (measure degeneracy, assess signal alignment, choose aggregation) provides a reusable protocol for structured test-time scaling (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Entity-level construction as a partial mitigation for structured test-time scaling failures.}\n    The diagnostic naturally motivates entity-level aggregation, adapting entity voting from ensemble NER \\cite{florian2003named} to stochastic sampling: under fine-tuning and zero-shot regimes, LP-weighted construction yields $+1.40$\\,pp on Few-NERD ($p{<}0.001$; survives Holm--Bonferroni, $k{=}57$) and $+1.02$\\,pp on SciERC ($p{=}0.004$, $p_{\\text{adj}}{=}0.20$; \\S\\ref{sec:prescriptive}).\n    Construction and LP selection are complementary, governed by LP-quality alignment.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n",
